" <p> The proposed increase would be phased in over time , with about an 11% increase in the first year , followed by roughly 4% increases in each of the next two years . <p> " A lot of what we 're trying to do is spread costs out over time , helping to avoid those spikes as much as we can , " Brooks said . <p> In response to calls for an audit , Brooks pointed to the existing regulatory process , which includes oversight from the Utilities Commission and public input . <p> Regulators will weigh those concerns in the months ahead , including during an expert witness hearing scheduled for August . <p> For customers planning to attend Monday 's hearing , the moment offers a rare opportunity to weigh @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pay for electricity and how those costs are shared for years to come . 
@@135830815 <p> New regulatory guidance for diesel engines could save U.S. industries billions of dollars for farmers and truckers , but clean-air advocates say it could pose a significant risk to public health . <p> Lee Zeldin , the head of the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency , was in Rocky Mount Monday to tout his agency 's decision last week to remove a requirement for exhaust fluid sensors on diesel equipment . <p> Other WRAL Top Stories <p> The move comes in response to concerns raised by farmers , truckers , motor coach operators and other diesel equipment operators over diesel exhaust sensor failures that can eat into productivity , the EPA says . <p> The U.S. Small Business Administration estimates that the new guidance will save farmers and truckers about $13.79 billion in repair costs and lost productivity . <p> " It 's about being responsive to the concerns of North Carolina families and farmers who have reached out to EPA , " Zeldin told reporters at the Rocky Mount Chamber of Commerce . <p> Diesel exhaust fluid is a substance used in diesel engines to reduce air pollution . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ compliance with clean-air regulations . <p> When DEF runs out or a sensor fails , it can cause a vehicle to reduce speed or become inoperable . That can cause disruptions in logistics , agriculture , and construction , regulators say . But DEF sensors are frequently faulty , according to the EPA . " It has caused needless frustration , operational delays , and real economic hardship for countless farmers , truckers , and equipment operators , " the agency said in a news release last week . <p> Industry groups have praised the move . <p> The American Trucking Associations called it a pragmatic solution . <p> " These systems have too often sidelined otherwise safe , compliant trucks due to faulty or unreliable DEF quality sensors , an issue that was compounded by widespread parts shortages in recent years , " Patrick Kelly , the associations ' vice president of energy and environmental affairs , said in a statement . " When a bad sensor can trigger a full inducement , the result is unnecessary downtime , unnecessary towing costs , strained supply chains , and higher @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , legislative director at the Climate Justice Alliance , says the change could harm public health and the environment . <p> " By revoking this , the administration continues to show that their priorities are grounded in sacrificing our health , endangering communities and commodifying the Earth for more corporate extraction and profits , at the expense of our future , " she said in a statement . <p> Other environmental-protection groups have also questioned the EPA 's move , calling on the agency to release data it collected to arrive at its decision to relax the regulations . " Public release of this information would help stakeholders understand the breadth and scope of DEF issues , " John . D. " Tariffs in select cases can be vital for tackling unfair trade practices , " Davis said . " However , the current wide-reaching and unpredictable approach has hurt farmers and manufacturers in eastern North Carolina . "

Jeff Landry and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the hunt for government actors who are wasting our money . <p> Well , they can easily spot the culprits in one of history 's largest wastes of public spending just by looking in a mirror . <p> Since 2007 , they have approved more than $21 billion in spending on our Master Plan for a Sustainable Coast . Using that funding , the Coastal Protection and Restoration Authority produced global-leading research and engineering on how to save and rebuild coastlines threatened by industrial destruction and the growing impacts of climate change . <p> And what have the governor and his allies done with those very expensive facts ? They 've mostly ignored and denied them -- and even worked against the solutions uncovered for our coastal salvation . <p> An example of that waste was recently highlighted in new research that made shocking headlines around the world : Current sea levels average eight inches to a foot higher worldwide -- and several feet higher in some areas -- than previously thought . That means surging sea levels caused by the fossil fuel emissions driving climate change will be drowning @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ news sent many governments rushing to find funding for emergency adaptations . <p> But Louisiana 's coastal research community was not panicked . <p> That 's because those new sea levels were largely a result of finding some coastline elevations had previously been overestimated , especially in poorer countries with less funding for scientific research . <p> So , the oceans are not higher , the land in some places is lower than thought . <p> Louisiana was not listed among suspect areas because we have the most accurate records of coastal elevation of any spot on the planet . For 20 years , the CPRA 's Coastwide Reference Monitoring System ( CRMS ) has used almost 400 stations across the coastal zone to constantly measure take ecological measurements including elevation and subsidence . <p> There is nothing like it anywhere in the world . <p> " I have realized for a long time what we have here is completely unparalleled , " said Tulane University professor and researcher Torbj ? rn T ? rnqvist . " What we have been able to do in Louisiana , we are not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world for at least the next 10 to 20 years . My colleagues even in western Europe are always amazed and quite envious of what we have to work with . " <p> Thanks to CRMS and other master plan research , Louisiana 's coastal zone is arguably the most understood in the world . And when that information is combined with the peer-reviewed findings on the dramatic acceleration of sea level rise , our governor and lawmakers are armed with the most accurate information of how to extend the lives of communities on our sediment-starved and sinking coastal landscapes -- the most productive and economically important part of the state . <p> The conclusions reached with the $21 billion the state has spent include the fact that emissions from the use of fossil fuels are causing the rapid rise of sea level that could drown much of the area below I-10 in the decades ahead . <p> The obvious and urgent response would be for Louisiana to join other states and nations in working to reduce the production and use of oil , gas and coal . <p> But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has devoted his entire political career in Congress , as our attorney general and now as governor to fighting those reforms because they might hurt oil and gas profits . <p> And he has killed the river sediment diversions in the master plan that the state-sponsored science has shown are the best way to ensure any long-term sustainability to some parts of our coastal zone . <p> This is a governor who was outraged at wasting $100 million on an LSU coaching mistake but seems to be OK throwing away the facts found about our coastal survival after spending $21 billion -- all because it might hurt the oil business he loves .

@@135861701 <p> The South Portland Comprehensive Plan Committee approved its version of the document last week . The proposal will go before the City Council for a public hearing on April 21 , and if approved , will be sent for state review . <h> What is the purpose of a comprehensive plan ? <p> A comprehensive plan is a long-term plan , a guide for city policy and growth for the next 15 years . <p> Committee members , including residents , elected officials and city staff , have met for three years to craft a vision for the future of the city . <p> The committee compiled and reviewed comments that community members left on the draft plan , spending February and March revising and updating . <h> How is the city promoting growth and economic transformation in this plan ? <p> Commercial growth would occur throughout the city . <p> Advertisement <p> " That does n't mean every street is going to become a storefront , " City Planner Milan Nevajda said . " But most of our corridors are going to be given a little bit more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the community . " <p> Higher traffic roads like Broadway , Ocean Street and Cottage Road would see more flexible zoning to support economic development . <p> Downtown Mill Creek has been identified as a spot for growth , with the opportunity for more multi-story , mixed-use development . <p> The western part of the city near the Maine Mall also has potential for commercial and residential growth , Nevajda said . There are a number of vacant parcels , and the comprehensive plan calls for flexible land use to promote significant investment . <h> Related <h> What 's the city 's plan to address housing needs ? <p> The median home price rose 56% between 2015 and 2021 , from $256,704 to $400,000 , adjusting for inflation , according to MaineHousing data . Rental costs have also increased : In 2012 , the median gross rent was $980 . In 2021 , it was $1,465 , a 49% increase . <p> Advertisement <p> South Portland needs more housing at all income levels , according to the 2022 city assessment . The rate of housing demand averages 54 year-round units @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 27 vacant units per year . <p> The city is willing to get creative to meet these needs , although what that housing looks like will depend on the area . <p> " All neighborhoods have to contribute to the housing solution . " Nevajda said . " Nobody gets a pass . " <p> A runner on South Portland 's Greenbelt Pathway passes an oil tank in South Portland . in 2021 . ( Michele McDonald/Staff Photographer ) Purchase this image <p> Areas on the western side of the city , including the West End Business Park , Sable Oaks Golf Course and Maine Mall , have been identified as locations for future housing stock , including multi-family builds . <h> Related <p> Both of these areas include parcels owned by the Portland Pipe Line Corp. , a subsidiary of the Canadian oil company , Suncor . But Nevajda said that the city can use the comprehensive plan as a way to set the city 's intentions . <p> Advertisement <p> The 100-acre tank farm in the middle of the Nutter Hill neighborhood could become a walkable neighborhood complete @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ periphery , SMRT Architects imagined in a community forum last April . <h> What is the city 's plan for transportation ? <p> The city wants to expand safe and reliable multi-modal transportation networks by 2040 . <p> It plans to enhance and expand off-street pathways , like the Greenbelt , to make it easier for residents to get across the city by bike or on foot . New land use regulations stipulate that developers will have to incorporate pedestrian and bike paths into their plans , and the city will also explore ways to bring more paths to the western part of the city . <p> The city will also be conducting roundabout feasibility studies for a number of known bottlenecks , like Cash Corner and the intersection of Sawyer and Broadway , and is requesting a bike and pedestrian master plan to look into ways to make intersections safer . <p> The city will have a traffic impact fee -- assessed based on the stress that new developments will place on existing infrastructure -- that will help fund intersection improvements . <p> The city is also interested in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and optimization as well as adding stops to parts of the city that can expect higher density . <p> Advertisement <h> How is the city planning for climate change impacts ? <p> The plan also proposes making South Portland a " climate-ready city " by 2040 . <p> The sea level in Maine is projected to rise between 1.1 and 3.2 feet by 2050 and 3 and 9.3 feet by 2100 , increasing the risks of coastal flooding . By 2050 , parts of Willard Beach and Ferry Village are expected to flood during a 10-year storm . <p> The plan proposes creating coastal resilience overlay zones that will dictate how future development will be built in at-risk areas . The building requirements would differ depending on land use . <p> " If you have a parking garage , you probably have to do very minimal things in order to operate in a flood risk area , " Nevajda said . " But if you had a hospital or some other community-sensitive use , like large amounts of housing , you 'll have to prepare differently , and generally that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than the water levels . " <p> High temperatures on the Sunday after the Fourth of July made for a busy day at Willard Beach in South Portland . ( Leslie Bridgers/Staff Writer ) <p> The plan stipulates that new development must minimize risk from at least 3.9 feet of sea level rise , plus a 100-year storm , or an extreme weather event with a 1% chance of occuring each year . For critical infrastructure and services like police and fire stations and sewage infrastructure , new builds or significant renovations must adhere to 8.8 feet of sea level rise , plus a 100-year storm . <p> In particularly high risk areas like Willard Beach and Ferry Village , the plan recommends suppressed development and in certain areas , voluntary relocation inland . <p> Near Willard Beach , the city is potentially exploring a transfer of development rights ordinance . If someone with waterfront property has a friend or neighbor who lives on a double lot further inland that was n't necessarily zoned for the construction of another home , they would be able to put another house on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ very at-risk area . <p> " It 's got to be a perfect scenario , " Nevajda said . " You have to have some connections , a willingness to have someone build a house on their property , the means to build the house and the ability to sell the property . " <p> Nevajda said that in the future , the city could consider an advocacy program reaching out to owners of double lots to see if they would be willing to help out neighbors in flood zones . <h> What 's happening at the eastern waterfront near Bug Light ? <p> The eastern waterfront , or the area between Front Street , Preble Street , Bug Light Park and the water , is recommended for rezoning , with residential use permitted in land not directly touching the water . <p> People wait Saturday morning to visit Portland Breakwater Lighthouse during Maine Open Lighthouse Day in South Portland on Sept. 13 . Portland Breakwater Lighthouse is commonly known as Bug Light , which is open to visitors two days each year . ( Daryn Slover/Staff Photographer ) Purchase @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ density use closer to the shore and higher density farther inland . Developers along the Fore River on either side of the zone are allowed to build to a maximum of three stories , with no residential . <p> Advertisement <p> Moving away from the water , the density increases to three , four and five stories , and residential is permitted . The section of land owned by the Portland Pipe Line Corp . -- including two tanks and an adjacent vacant field -- could support buildings up to six stories . <p> Some community members have been adamantly against any residential development near Bug Light , citing environmental and traffic concerns . Sea level rise could inundate this area in the future , and it 's near petroleum tanks . <p> Dana Richie is a community reporter covering South Portland and Cape Elizabeth . " The environmentalists , I mean , they are terrorists , " he said . " They were terrorists . I call them the environmental terrorists . " <p> Even as he spoke , dozens of oil and gas fields , pipelines , refineries and export terminals lay damaged across the Middle East , thanks to the war Trump and Israel launched more than a month earlier . This infrastructure will take years to fully restore . <p> Iran has responded to the war by also clamping down on the Strait of Hormuz , cutting off more than 20 million barrels per day of global oil and refined-products supply , according to my Bloomberg Opinion colleague Javier Blas , along with a fifth of the world 's supply of liquefied natural gas . Stockpile releases , rerouting oil through pipelines to the Red Sea and other emergency offsets have made up some of that shortfall . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shy of the amount of dirty fuel to which its economy has grown accustomed . <p> The longer the war continues , the more economically painful the loss of those barrels will be . After governments have exhausted emergency relief measures , the next strategy is squeezing demand for oil . This process has already begun in Asian and African economies that depend most heavily on fuel from the Middle East . The Philippines has declared four-day workweeks . Pakistanis are watching cricket matches on TV instead of driving to them . South Sudan is rationing electricity . <p> Like the demand destruction that accompanied the outbreak of COVID , this will grow and spread around the world as long as the proximate cause -- in this case , the energy shock caused by the Iran war -- persists . The risk is another global recession . <p> And , just like COVID 's brief plunge in economic activity and greenhouse-gas emissions , the Iran war 's hit to energy demand wo n't be permanent , unless we all agree to live in a donkey-cart-based economy . As long as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is for people and governments to explore energy alternatives . <p> In many cases , unfortunately , they 'll fall back to burning coal , still an accessible and localized fuel . But in many other cases , they 'll turn to the renewable sources that are proliferating exponentially around the world . Far more of those are available , and at much cheaper prices , than during the oil crisis of the 1970s , which first shocked the world into recognizing the need for alternatives . And the economic security renewables provide in this world of uncertain geopolitics is sure to herald a new wave of investments into the sector in fossil-fuel-poor economies . <p> " The main message is that we 're going to get the energy transition forced on us in a very painful way that 's going to happen very quickly , " Jeff Currie , chief strategy officer of energy pathways at Carlyle Group Inc. , told Bloomberg News . <p> That ca n't be the outcome Trump expected or wanted when he launched this war . More than simply calling environmentalists bad names , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environmental regulations , clean energy and climate science . Every member of his cabinet sings loudly from the same hymnal , which holds that carbon emissions are n't a problem and clean energy is a waste of money that makes all energy unaffordable . <p> The irony is that Trump 's war has revealed just how unaffordable fossil fuels can get , and how elusive his desired " energy dominance " is when a country remains yoked to globally traded fuels , as my colleague Liam Denning has noted . <p> It 's silly to talk of silver linings given the war 's cost in human casualties and economic and geopolitical stability , along with the lasting environmental scars inflicted by oil and gas burning across the region . In no way could you call this war " worth it , " from a climate or any other perspective . Far better that it ends today . But if it must go on , then we have to hope that it will at least hasten a new global energy system that makes future wars over oil and other resources less @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the author and does not necessarily reflect the opinion of the editorial board or Bloomberg LP and its owners . @@135865411 <p> Senate President Mattie Daughtry , D-Brunswick , presides during a session Monday at the Maine State House in Augusta . ( Joe Phelan/Staff Photographer ) <p> Maine lawmakers Tuesday took a possible step toward establishing a program to recoup money from large , corporate polluters to pay for the impacts of climate change . <p> The House of Representatives passed a bill that would study how much greenhouse gas emissions have cost the state in a 75-72 vote that fell largely along party lines . Two Democrats , Tiffany Roberts of South Berwick and Holly Sargent of York , joined with Republicans in opposition . <p> The Senate passed the bill 19-13 earlier in the week with Democrats in support and Republicans opposed . <p> The bill , LD 1870 , sponsored by Sen. Stacy Brenner , D-Scarborough , started out as a proposal for a so-called " climate superfund " that would collect fees from groups that extract fossil fuels and refine crude oil and whose activities are found to contribute to certain levels of greenhouse gas emissions . The money would be used to fund climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this week , however , was revised to a proposal for a study of the cost to the state of greenhouse gas emissions from 1995 to 2024 . The information is set to be submitted to lawmakers in a report due by Jan. 1 , 2028 . <h> Related <p> Supporters say the revised version represents an important first step toward holding polluters accountable with less potential for legal ramifications . Laws that are similar to Brenner 's original proposal are currently being challenged with lawsuits from businesses and the Trump administration in Vermont and New York . <p> Advertisement <p> In the Vermont case , the U.S. Justice Department has argued that the state law is unconstitutional and represents an unlawful attempt to regulate emissions that cross state lines -- which it says is the job of the federal , not state , government , the New York Times reported this week . <p> Sen. Denise Tepler , D-Topsham , who chairs the Environment and Natural Resources Committee , said Maryland has taken a more incremental approach to establishing a climate superfund program . Lawmakers there passed a bill @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Tepler suggested Maine should do the same thing . <p> " LD 1870 will allow Maine to assess the true cost to our state of climate pollution as we watch the lawsuits unfold in New York and Vermont , " Tepler said on the Senate floor Monday . <p> The proposal still needs to go through second votes in both chambers before it can be sent to Gov . Janet Mills for her approval . It also comes with a $600,000 price tag and will have to compete with other bills for funding . <p> A spokesperson for Mills said Tuesday that the governor supports reducing greenhouse gas emissions to mitigate the effects of climate change , and that she will review the bill if it reaches her desk . <p> The measure drew close to 45 minutes of debate in the House on Tuesday . Democrats argued the study is an important step toward mitigating damages from climate change impacts . They pointed to a string of severe winter storms that hit the state in late 2023 and early 2024 . <p> The money for the study would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with the establishment of a climate superfund program , they said . <p> Republicans noted opposition from business groups to a climate superfund , said the study was unnecessary and cited the legal challenges that have arisen in other states . <p> " This amendment looks like a retreat , but it is not , " said Rep. Mike Soboleski , R-Phillips . " It is a strategic delay that sets the stage for the same policy fights , only now they will fall on the next Legislature and the next administration .
